It contradicted the invariant written at the top of that section: *the state before save N is by definition the state after save N-1*. The clone now happens **inside the chain, immediately before `api.saveShots`**, for the same reason the revision has always been read there. `bornAt` deliberately stays at queue time and is now commented as such: it is the generation the gesture was *made* in, and a history discard between the click and the send is exactly what it exists to notice — read at send time it would adopt the post-discard generation and agree with itself.

**Finding 6 — a refused playhead snap still wrote.** `moved` was computed *before* `applyPlayheadSnap`, which on refusal restores the shot from `original` and answers `false`. Control fell into `if (moved) saveShotsSilently("resize")` anyway, so a snap refused by `cutMoveRefusal` — locked, approved, or an in-flight neighbour — issued a no-op `PUT /shots`: it bumped `updated_at`, pushed a phantom undo entry for a gesture that never happened, and threw the redo stack away, immediately after telling the Director the edit was refused. `moved` is now re-read after each step. A second, smaller leak came out of the same fix: `move` sets the dirty flag on the first pixel and every drag used to end in a write, so a gesture that now ends without one would have left the flag standing and had the project-switch guard asking about work that does not exist. It is put back where `saveShotsSilently` puts it.

**Finding 5 — a drag left its press standing as half a double-click.** `lastEdgePress` was set on every resize-handle `pointerdown` and cleared only when a double-press completed; nothing cleared it on move or up, and `doubleEdgePress` measures from the **first** press. A 300 ms edge drag followed by re-grabbing the same edge 100 ms later fell inside `EDGE_DOUBLE_CLICK_MS` and ran the gap fill instead of starting the second drag, stretching the shot to its neighbour. The release now consumes the press when the pointer actually travelled, decided by `edgePressSurvivesDrag` against a documented three-pixel slop — a real double-click on a 7 px handle does not travel, and a hand that jitters a pixel between clicks is still double-clicking. The measurement is the furthest the pointer got from where it went down, not where it ended: a drag that wandered out and came back has still been a drag.

**Finding 3 — the overwrite consent was unreachable and the skip reasons were thrown away.** The section inspector's button bailed with `if (!report.filled) return toast(report.message, "error")` **before** it ever asked `FILL_SECTION_LOOKS_OVERWRITE_QUESTION`. A structure where every section already carries a look short-circuits server-side to `SECTION_LOOKS_ALL_WRITTEN` with `filled: 0` — so for the Director's live project, which has all seven written, the button could do nothing *but* error, while the sentence it showed ("send overwrite=true to replace what is there") described something the screen had no way to do. The same early return discarded the per-section `SECTION_LOOK_SKIP_UNDESCRIBED` reasons whenever the model filled nothing, which the route's docstring calls half the feature. The handler now asks the consent exactly where it belongs — on a report that filled nothing but holds looks the Director wrote — and **answering yes re-reports with `overwrite: true` rather than applying blind**, so the confirm still previews what would land and still carries that plan back under the same-day `plan_id` contract. That costs the same two model calls as the ordinary path (the all-written report is answered without a model call at all) and it keeps the property the confirm change exists for: what lands is what a person read. The report itself is now **held and drawn in the panel** (`sectionLooksReportLines`, `.snap-report` reused from the cuts bar, dismissable) instead of living only inside a dialog nobody can re-read, and each line names the words a look would replace beside the words it would write — "6 filled, 1 left alone" is not a sentence anybody can consent to.

**What was driven in a real browser.** `tests/e2e_timeline_edit.py` gained three sections and `tests/e2e_section_looks.py` is new. Finding 4: `PUT /shots` is held open for 2.5 s by a latency shim over `window.fetch` (the application's own code path is untouched — `request()` looks `fetch` up by name), a split and an add are clicked while the first write is still open, the run asserts an open write existed at the moment of the second gesture, and then that **one Undo restores exactly the 13-shot plan between them** and the second reaches the 12-shot seed, with the button naming "adding a shot" then "the split". Finding 5: two real drags in **one** W3C action sequence, with a capture-phase recorder proving the presses were 162 ms apart — inside the 400 ms window read out of `api.js` in the page — and the shot ending at 6.71 s rather than stretched to its neighbour at 27.517 s; then a genuine double-click on the same edge, which still fills the gap to exactly 27.517 s. Finding 6: a left-edge drag onto a parked playhead across the cut shared with a locked shot, refused in `SNAP_LOCKED_REFUSAL`'s own words, with **zero** `PUT /shots` (2 before, 2 after), `updated_at` unchanged, both history buttons byte-identical before and after, and a project switch that does not ask about unsaved work. Finding 3: a project shaped like the Director's — seven sections, all written, a real treatment and style bible — where the consent is asked, declined, and nothing goes on the wire; then accepted, previewed against the existing text, applied, and read back off disk with the one section the pass had nothing to say about left exactly as written; then a *mixed* structure, where the preview comes first, the consent is the second question, and one report and one confirm — one model call — fill the empty boxes and replace the written ones; then a structure with nothing written and nothing answerable, where no consent is asked and all seven skip sentences are on screen. **No GPU or model time was spent**: the language-model host for that run is a stub HTTP server the script starts, `MVP_LLM_BASE_URL` is pointed at it before the app boots (environment beats `.env` in pydantic-settings), and the stub counts its calls — which is how the run asserts the confirming call asks no model at all.

**Twelve mutations, every one killed, restores verified by SHA-256 on a CRLF tree with the anchor match count asserted before any write.** Six die offline in `tests/test_frontend_contract.py` — the queue-time snapshot (as master's own two lines, applied as a pair because either half alone is a no-op the send-time assignment overwrites), `edgePressSurvivesDrag` always false, the Finding 3 early return, `sectionLooksWritten` always false, the report not drawn in the panel, and the diff clause dropped from a line. **Four can only be killed by a browser**, which is the point: measuring `moved` once, keeping the press across a drag, leaving the dirty flag standing, and suppressing the consent on a mixed structure — that last one is a `window.confirm` on the second of two paths, and no offline test moved at all. As a check on the mutations themselves, master's own `app.js`/`api.js` were checked out and the harness run against them: sections 1–6 pass and the new section 7 fails with *"one Undo rolled back both gestures at once"*. Four new executed-contract cases cover the pure halves offline. Suite: 1392 passing, ruff clean, `node --check` clean on both modules.

**Found, not fixed.** A drag that wanders further than the slop and returns to exactly where it started now consumes its press, so an immediate re-grab starts a drag rather than filling a gap — deliberate (it was a drag) but worth naming. `applyPlayheadSnap` still calls `saveShotsSilently` itself on the success path rather than answering what to save, which is why the refusal had to be re-read rather than simply returned. And the section-look report is whole-structure while it is drawn inside whichever section box is selected; selecting a different box shows the same report, which is correct but not obvious from the panel.

## 2026-08-21 — "Report then confirm" applied text nobody read: the confirm now carries the report, and 1 of 24 rewrites proved it mattered

**Two findings from a review over `d6d2e3a..21df7f3`, and the first was confirmed live before it was fixed.** `fill_section_looks` and `clean_shot_prompts` both called the model **unconditionally, before** looking at `confirm_apply`. A client therefore had to call twice — once for the report a person reads, once to apply — and *each call generated independently at `PLAN_TEMPERATURE = 0.7`*. The text applied was not the text reported. Measured that night on the Director's real 33-shot plan: the prose-cleanup report was run, all 24 proposed rewrites were read and approved, and **one landed as different text** — `shot_f7296453a359` reviewed as "Extreme close up of smeared crimson lips, wet." and applied as "Extreme close up of crimson lips, smeared and wet.". Both readings happen to be fine, which is luck; the next run could put prose nobody read onto hand-reviewed text. It also doubled the cost of a call that takes up to 300 s on the local model, and it made the docstrings' central safety claim ("this report exists to be read by a person before it lands") false.

**The fix is that the confirm carries the report back, whole.** `plan` on each request is the response model of the same route, so the client echoes the body it was given rather than reconstructing something the server would have to trust. Two things make the echo as strong as a server-side cache: `plan_id`, a SHA-256 the **server** mints over the report it emitted and recomputes over the report it is handed — any changed field of any row fails it; and `updated_at`, the report's project revision, checked against the live one and refused with `PROJECT_CHANGED_REFUSAL`, the wording `replace_project` and `replace_shots` already use. `updated_at` is *inside* the digest as well as compared against it, so a client that edits the revision to get past the 409 fails the digest instead. A confirm that carries no plan is refused (`CLEAN_PROMPTS_NO_PLAN` / `SECTION_LOOKS_NO_PLAN`) rather than served by generating; a confirm whose plan does not match is refused (`..._PLAN_MISMATCH`) rather than regenerating, because regenerating is the defect. **No model is called on the confirm path at all**, which is the cost half of the finding and is asserted against a recording double.

**Echo rather than a cache keyed by plan id, weighed both ways.** No server state means no lifetime, no eviction, no "which worker holds it", and a plan survives a restart the way a shot list does. It is this codebase's existing idiom rather than a new one — `ShotListRequest` is already a full payload plus a revision token for exactly this reason, and the refusal wording already exists. It makes the guarantee checkable from the wire alone: the report body and the confirm body can be compared byte for byte by anything holding both. And a cache would still need the revision check, so it is strictly more machinery for the same promise, with one extra failure mode — plan evicted — whose only remedy is spending the 300 s call again. The digest is a plain hash, not a keyed MAC, and that is honest about what it defends: the measured hazard is a client that *regenerates*, not an attacker, and a client that recomputes the digest over substituted text is asserting "this is what I reviewed" as deliberately as any `PUT /shots` asserts its body.

**`overwrite` is deliberately outside the plan and outside its digest.** It is a consent given *after* the report is read, and the report already carries the proposed look on a written section's row — so the second question is answered against the plan rather than by reading the treatment a second time. `SectionLooksResponse` gained `stray` for the same reason: it is the one number the summary sentence says and the rows cannot, so a confirm that changes the counts can rebuild that sentence exactly instead of losing its last clause. The message is rebuilt *only* when `overwrite` actually moved the counts, so an ordinary confirm returns the reported message byte for byte, `SECTION_LOOKS_ALL_WRITTEN` included.

**Finding two: a hand edit during the model call was silently overwritten.** `clean_shot_prompts` re-reads the project after the await and recomputes `labels` and `before` from the **fresh** prompt — but `answered[shot.id]` is a rewrite of the **stale** one, and the stale rewrite was what got written. The Director edits shot 12 while the call runs and their sentence is replaced by a revision of the sentence they deleted. The prompt each answer was produced from is now kept across the await and compared; a shot whose prompt moved is dropped and named with `CLEAN_PROMPTS_EDITED`, this codebase's convention that a named skip beats a silent guess, and re-asking would spend the call again. **The docstring at that re-read claimed the opposite** — "a prompt edited by hand in that window is re-examined on its new text" — describing behaviour the code never had; it now says what the code does, and says what it used to claim.

**Tests, and what had to change.** Six new cases plus two forced-guard cases: the confirm applies byte-identical text through a fresh `ProjectStore` against a **drifting** double that answers differently on every call (a double returning identical prose cannot tell a route that re-asks from one that does not, so every existing test would have passed against the broken code); the model is called once on the report and not at all on the confirm; absent, tampered, forged and stale plans each refuse and write nothing, and none re-asks; a prompt hand-edited between ask and apply is skipped, named, and left exactly as typed while the other 23 shots still apply; the overwrite consent is taken against the report in one model call; and each route's row-level check is forced to fire by standing in a digest that accepts anything, `CLEAN_PROMPTS_WINDOWS_MOVED`'s own test's method. **Existing tests changed rather than being quietly edited: 12 call sites in `test_prompt_cleanup.py` and 10 in `test_api.py` moved from `confirm_apply=True` in one call to report-then-confirm helpers (`clean_applying`, `fill_looks_applying`), because a single confirming call generating its own text is precisely the shape being removed.** `comfy.prompts` is empty on every path.

**Fourteen mutations, every one killed.** Each of the two routes' absent-plan refusal, revision check, digest check, row check and confirm branch; the hand-edit skip; the rewritten-only filter; the overwrite consent; and the message-rebuild condition. No survivors, restores verified by SHA-256. Suite: 1392 passing, ruff clean, `node --check` clean on both modules.

**One test rewritten, and it is worth naming.** `test_the_directors_live_project_is_never_written_by_this_test_module` asserted that the live manifest still held 33 shots and 24 echoing prompts — so it went red the moment the Director applied this very feature to it, and would have gone red again on their next timeline edit. The intent was right; the mechanism coupled a test to the *content* of a file the Director changes by doing their job, and a bytes-unchanged snapshot would have been just as wrong, since a running batch writes job records into that manifest. The guarantee is now enforced at the seam instead: an autouse fixture guards `ProjectStore.project_dir` so any test in that module reaching inside the live data root fails inside the call that reached, naming itself. Nothing asserts what the file contains.

**Found, not fixed.** If the Director edits the *treatment* while `fill_section_looks` is reading it, the report proposes looks derived from the pre-edit text. A person still reads and confirms exactly what lands, so the guarantee this change is about holds — but the material the model read is stale, and there is no `CLEAN_PROMPTS_EDITED` equivalent for a document. Separately, the project id inside the digest defends against replaying a plan at another project; no test forces that branch, because the revision check catches it first with a better sentence.

## 2026-08-21 — The manifest lock slept through its own backoff, so the poll it was added to protect was the thing it stalled

**The finding,** from a review over `d6d2e3a..21df7f3`: `_replace_atomically` runs inside `with _MANIFEST_LOCK` and `time.sleep`s up to half a second across its retries *while holding it*. `store.get` and `store.save` are called straight out of `async def` routes — `read_render_status`, `generate_h3`, `fill_section_looks`, `clean_shot_prompts`, `director_chat` — so the sleep was not merely blocking the manifest being written. It was blocking the event loop, and with it every unrelated request, `/render-status` included. The lock exists to keep that two-second poll from breaking saves; sleeping under it made the lock break the poll. A 33-shot batch polling every two seconds is exactly the shape that meets it.

**The fix is one line, plus the object that makes it correct.** The module gained `_MANIFEST_WAIT = threading.Condition(_MANIFEST_LOCK)` — a condition built over the *same* lock, not a second one — and the backoff became `_MANIFEST_WAIT.wait(...)` instead of `time.sleep(...)`. `Condition.wait` releases the lock for the duration and re-acquires it before returning, so the process is free while a save waits on a foreign handle. Every replace *attempt* is still made with the lock held, which is the entire guard: unchanged. The reason it must be a `Condition` and not a bare `release()`/`acquire()` pair is `create`, which calls `save` while already holding the lock — the lock is reentrant and stands at depth two there. `Condition.wait` drops an `RLock` to its full recursion depth and restores that depth; a single `release()` would leave the outer acquisition standing and the stall entirely intact.

**Can releasing between attempts reintroduce the original race? No, and the reason is where the handle lives.** The original bug was a *reader* holding `project.json` open at the instant of `os.replace`. A reader only ever has that file open while holding `_MANIFEST_LOCK` — `get` takes the lock, calls `read_text`, and releases; the parse happens after. So a reader admitted during the backoff has opened and closed the file, and holds nothing, before the retry can re-acquire and attempt again. The attempt still runs under the lock, so no in-process handle can coexist with it. What the window genuinely admits is a *writer*: another complete `save` can land its own manifest mid-backoff, and the retry then replaces it. That is last-writer-wins, which is already the store's semantics — every route reads, mutates and saves as three separate acquisitions with the mutation outside the lock — and both manifests are whole at every instant. What it does *not* admit is a partial manifest, because each interleaved call is itself atomic under the same lock. Running store I/O off the loop with `to_thread` was the other suggested fix and was rejected: it touches `app.py`, it leaves the lock still held across a sleep, and it converts a loop stall into a threadpool pile-up rather than removing it.

**The stall test asserts order, not elapsed time.** A timing assertion here would flake and be blamed on some unrelated change a month from now. Instead the first `Path.replace` is made to fail, and a second thread — running the real `store.get`, not a bare acquire — takes the lock during the backoff, records itself, and `notify_all`s to end the wait. The lock itself guarantees the ordering: the retry cannot resume until the reader lets go, so a correct implementation records `["replace", "reader", "replace"]` and a version that sleeps under the lock records the reader last. The five-second backoff is not a measurement; it is only the bound on how long the broken version takes to fail. Two more tests beside it: a full `save` interleaved into another save's backoff, asserting the interleaved manifest is on disk whole and that the retry's content is what finally lands with no orphaned temp file; and the same stall check under `create`, which additionally proves the recursive release came back balanced by having a fresh thread take the lock uncontended afterwards.

**Five mutations, each killed.** Reverting the backoff to `time.sleep` under the lock fails all three new tests. Removing the lock from `get` and `save` fails the original reproduction — the guard was not hollowed out. Moving the replace attempt itself outside the lock while keeping the released backoff fails that same reproduction, which is the check that matters most here. `RLock` → `Lock` fails the deadlock test. Dropping the temp-file unlink on the give-up path fails the debris test. Suite: 1386 passing, ruff clean.

**Found, not fixed.** `save` still holds the lock across the temp file's `write`, `flush` and `fsync`. Nothing reads that file — it has a unique name — so the lock buys nothing there, and an `fsync` is the one genuinely slow call under it. Narrowing it to the replace alone is a separate change with its own reasoning about `create`'s existence check, and was left out of a fix aimed at one finding.

## 2026-08-21 — The reader was breaking the writer: `store.get` made `store.save` fail on Windows, and a lost save is a lost render record

**The report,** from an agent driving a real browser: *"`store.get` and `store.save` race on Windows. My first harness polled the manifest and was breaking the writes it waited for."* `store.py` was not that agent's surface, but the Director's browser polls `/render-status` every two seconds, so it is reachable in ordinary use — and an unattended overnight batch is exactly the shape that reaches it.

**The direction is the whole point and it is backwards from the intuition.** The save was already atomic: temp file, `flush`, `fsync`, `os.replace`. What nothing guarded was the *destination*. Windows refuses `MoveFileEx` onto a path any handle has open, and an open handle is precisely what a reader is, so a `get` landing inside a `save` did not produce a torn read — it produced `PermissionError: [WinError 5]` **out of the save**. A dropped read is one poll the browser retries two seconds later. A dropped save is a job that never settled, an output that never landed, a take that was never recorded.

**Reproduced, before anything was written.** Four reader threads against one writer, on a 33-shot 52 KB manifest, twelve seconds:

```
reads ok=40727 errors=102
saves ok=108   errors=3245      <- 97% of saves failed
leftover temp files: 3245
```

That last line is the same bug's second half, and it was not in the report: every failed replace abandoned its `NamedTemporaryFile` beside the manifest. Twelve seconds of contention left 3,245 files in the project directory.

**In-process, not cross-process** — established rather than assumed. FastAPI runs `def` endpoints in a threadpool and `async def` endpoints on the loop, and this application mixes them freely: `read_project` and `select_shot_take` are sync, `read_render_status` is async, and all three touch the store. Tracing `ProjectStore.get` through eight concurrent ASGI requests names both `MainThread` and `AnyIO worker thread`, so the collision needs no second process. That is what makes a `threading.RLock` sufficient, and it is why no file-lock dependency was added.

**The fix.** One module-level `threading.RLock` in `store.py`, taken by `get`, `save`, `list` and `create`. On the module and not on the instance, because two `ProjectStore` objects can address one data root and an instance lock would let them race. Reentrant because `create` calls `save` under it. Held only across the bytes that touch the disk: `model_dump_json` happens before the lock and `model_validate_json` after it, and `list` takes it per manifest rather than around the whole sweep, so a full data root never holds a render's save off for the length of a listing. Uncontended cost is inside the noise — 125.1 µs/get with, 126.5 µs/get without, on a 24-shot manifest — which matters because every route reads and the poll reads every two seconds.

**Two things the lock cannot do, done separately.** It cannot reach a handle this process does not own — a virus scanner, the search indexer, a second app instance on the same data root — so `_replace_atomically` retries the replace five times over half a second and then gives the caller the error rather than hanging. And a save that gives up now unlinks its own temp file, which is what kills the 3,245-file leak. Retry alone was rejected as the fix: under sustained polling the contention is continuous, and a bounded retry against a continuous collision just loses later.

**Deadlock analysis.** The lock is held only across filesystem calls and never across a network call, a model call or a callback — nothing under it re-enters application code. The single nesting in the codebase is `create` → `save`, which the reentrant lock permits; routes load, mutate and save as three separate acquisitions with the mutation outside. Swapping `RLock` for `Lock` deadlocks on the first `create`, and the test says so.

**Six tests, all mutation-checked.** The reproduction (three reader threads against sixty saves; zero save failures, zero read failures, no temp-file debris) fails against the pre-fix store and fails again when the lock is removed from *either* `get` or `save` alone. It can only ever flake toward passing, never toward a false failure, which is the property that matters for a test that will otherwise be blamed for the next unrelated change. Beside it: atomicity under concurrent writes (a reader sees one whole version or another, never a splice); the nesting/deadlock case, run in a worker thread so a hang fails on a join timeout instead of hanging the suite; the bounded retry; the give-up path leaving no debris and still raising; and an uncontended-read budget deliberately loose enough to catch only a future fix that puts a file lock or a sleep on the read path. Five mutations — unlocked `get`, unlocked `save`, no retry, no temp cleanup, non-reentrant lock — were each caught.

**What is still unprotected.** Two OS processes sharing one data root are serialised by nothing here; the retry makes their replaces survive each other, but a simultaneous save from each is still last-write-wins, exactly as it was before. Nothing in the application does that today (one `ProjectStore` per process, constructed once in `create_app`), and browser QA runs on an isolated data root. If that ever changes, the answer is a lock file, not a longer retry. Suite: 1377 passing.

## 2026-08-21 — The asset labels were in the prose because naming them *was* the citation; a field to cite from, and a pass to clean up after

**The report.** From the Director, on `project_59f14d19ff10`: the shot prompts read like an inventory rather than like direction.

> *"Extreme close up of **Crimson Lips Close-up** while HarderFaster performs."*
> *"**Blue Haze Atmosphere** surrounding the **Dusk Warehouse Bed**."*

The first says "close up of … Close-up". Measured over that plan: **24 of its 33 prompts** name a non-character asset. Not all of them are bad — "HarderFaster arches sensually on the Dusk Warehouse Bed" reads as a location — so the target is bad echoes, not all mentions.

**The cause is mechanical, not stylistic.** `populate` created a citation by scanning the prompt for asset display names; the previous entry's naming-leak fix bounded *which* names were offered but left the mechanism intact. So naming the asset in the sentence **was** the way to attach the picture: a shot that wanted the microphone had to type "Vintage Chrome Mic" into the text a renderer would later read. The prose was the attachment mechanism, and the wording was what it cost.

### Half A — a structural place to cite from

`director.PlannedShot.assets: list[str]`, promoted into the constrained decoder's `required` by `PLANNED_SHOT_DECISIONS` alongside `performance`. This is the **sixth** time the same hole has been found: a Pydantic field with any default is absent from `model_json_schema()["required"]`, that schema is what rides `response_format: json_schema strict` to LM Studio's decoder, and a caller that cannot proceed without a field must say so in the grammar (`shots`, `sections`, `PlannedShot.performance`, `PlannedSection.prompt`, `stage_manager.assets`, `SectionLook.prompt` — now this). Required does not mean "cite something": the empty list is legal and is a shot that uses nothing. It means the decoder may not close a shot object without having considered the question.

Entries resolve **by id or by exact display name**, case-insensitive and space-stripped, because those are the two spellings the model was shown — the instruction's roster is by name, the project-context dump beside it carries ids. An entry matching neither is **dropped**, never guessed at: it cannot invent an asset, and guessing which asset a near-miss meant is how a shot ends up conditioned on the wrong face.

**The prose scan is kept and demoted, and that is the load-bearing decision.** A model that writes a name and omits the field has said unambiguously which picture it wants; dropping that citation would send the shot to render with no reference at all — invisible until the take comes back wrong. An awkward sentence is reviewable by a human; a missing reference is not. **Losing a citation is the worse failure**, so `models.assets_for_proposal` runs declared-first, then the scan over whatever the field did not already name, with a shared `seen` set so an asset named both ways is cited once. Order is deterministic: declared entries in the model's own order, then un-declared prose matches in library order.

**Byte-identity is the compatibility argument and it is pinned.** With `assets` absent — a double written before today, or a provider that ignores the strict schema — `assets_for_proposal` returns exactly what the old expression returned: same assets, same order, same `order` values, so such a plan's citations and prose are what they have always been. The test asserts that against the old expression *spelled out in the test*, not against a helper, so a change to the helper cannot redefine what "what it always did" means. The chat route's schema is untouched: `assets` carries a default, so `director_result_schema()` is still byte-identical to `DirectorResult.model_json_schema()` and its pinned test passes unedited.

The instruction now asks for the names in `assets` and tells the prose to name nothing, with the Director's own two bad sentences quoted in it as the examples — this model family follows examples and ignores abstractions, measured five ways in this project. **One exception is carved explicitly:** a character asset's name is the performer's name and stays in the prose, because "she sings into the lens" with no subject is worse prose than the echo. `POPULATE_LOCATION_LINE` moved the same way — the declared location is listed in `assets` and described in the shot's own words.

### Half B — cleaning the plan that already exists

`POST /api/projects/{id}/timeline/clean-prompts`, report first and apply on confirm — `snap_timeline_cuts`' shape, `populate`'s `confirm_replace` at bottom. Without `confirm_apply` the route does not call `store.save` and the response carries no project at all, so "nothing was written" is visible on the wire. Every row carries the old prose beside the proposed prose, because this report exists to be read by a person before it lands.

**The windows are sacred, and that dominated every other decision here.** The Director: *"I have done some timeline touch ups and am generally happy for now."* Those 33 edges are hand-placed against musical timing and include six deliberate micro-cuts — 0.5 s, 1.75 s, 1.833 s, 1.875 s, 2.083 s, 2.667 s — that no tiling function would ever produce. **Repopulating is forbidden and nothing here proposes a window, an order or a count.** Three things make that a guarantee rather than an intention:

* the write loop assigns `Shot.prompt` and nothing else — no branch reads or writes `start`, `duration`, `citations`, `singing`, `use_song_audio`, `seed`, `mode`, `status`, `locked`, `latest_output`, `approved_output` or the takes;
* `prompt_cleanup.window_fingerprint` — every shot's `(id, start, duration)` in **manifest** order, compared whole so the count and the ordering are pinned too — is taken before the rewrites are applied and compared after, and a mismatch **refuses with a 500 and never calls `store.save`**. That is a check on data, where "the code only assigns one field" is a claim about code. Manifest order rather than song order because `shot_label` numbers the timeline by manifest position, so a reorder renames every clip on screen even when no window moved;
* `citation_fingerprint` is compared per shot across the same boundary, because the citations are precisely what makes removing a name from the prose safe: the ids are already on the shot, in roles, with orders, so the sentence never had to carry them. A cleanup that dropped one would have removed its own justification.

Both guards are unreachable by construction, so both are forced to fire in tests by standing in a fingerprint function that reports a drift; the route must then refuse **and leave the manifest untouched, prose included**.

**What counts as an echo.** A citable asset's display name in the prose, for every kind except `character` — the Director's own line, since the measurement is "24 of 30 prompts echo a **non-character** asset name" and their quoted bad sentence is bad in its first half only. The check is **case-sensitive**, and that was found by measurement rather than argued: a case-insensitive rule rejected 6 of the 24 rewrites and every one of them was *correct* — "Flickering light glints off the silver spiked choker" and "HarderFaster sings into the vintage chrome microphone" both still contain their label case-insensitively, and both are exactly the prose this pass exists to produce. What makes a phrase read like a catalogue entry is that it is capitalised as a name; lowercasing it into an ordinary noun phrase is a fix. The consequence, stated: a prompt that already spells a name in another case still *cites* that asset — the citation scan is case-insensitive — and is not reported as an echo. The citation is stored and is not this pass's to change.

**A shot with no echo is not sent to the model at all** and is reported as already clean. Asking for a rewrite of a prompt nobody complained about is how a hand-reviewed plan quietly acquires 33 changes when it needed 24.

**Two protections, in the existing words.** `EXPANSION_LOCKED_NOTICE` for a lock — the sentence expansion already refuses a prompt rewrite with, which is exactly the act being refused here — and `EXPANSION_RENDERED_NOTICE` for a render **in flight**, of whose two arms only that one is honoured: a job executing right now was submitted with this prompt, so rewriting it underneath would leave the record describing a submission that never happened. Neither shot is sent to the model.

**A rendered or approved shot is rewritten, and told about.** `shot_write_refusal` is deliberately not the gate, exactly as it is not the gate for `replace_asset_citations`, and the Director's ruling there transfers with room to spare: a citation change on a rendered shot is fine because the take is untouched, and prose is *less* coupled to a take than a citation is — the prompt each take was actually submitted with is recorded on its job and in the take's own PNG metadata, so editing the shot's text loses no record. `expansion_write_refusal` already carves the same exemption. What survives is the report: those shots are counted and named before the confirm, and no path writes `approved_output`, `approved_start` or `approved_duration`, so AD-13's staleness comparison and assembly read exactly what they read before.

The rewrite rides `director.expand`'s wire and `ShotExpansion`'s contract, selected by system prompt — the DP pass's own idiom, because "revised prompts addressed by shot id" is exactly this pass's output shape too. No new director method, no new schema, and every existing `expand` double keeps working. The payload is trimmed the way `dp_input` is: treatment, style bible, and per shot only its id, its prompt, the labels it must lose, and its section. **No asset roster travels** — a roster here would be a fresh list of names to echo, which is the defect in a new room — and no window travels, because this pass may not retime and does not need to know.

Four ways an answer is refused and reported rather than written: empty, JSON-as-prose, a rewrite that still contains the label, and a **gutting** — shorter than half the original with a 20-character floor, which catches the local failure where a model "removes the label" by returning the three words either side of it. An unanswered shot, a duplicated id (first answer wins) and a stray id addressed to no shot are each reported; a stray id is never created into a shot, because that would invent a window.

### What is not verified

**No live model has validated any of the rewrite wording.** `PROMPT_CLEANUP_SYSTEM_PROMPT` has never been sent to `gemma-4-26b-a4b-it-heretic-ara-v2` or to anything else; every test here answers with text a human wrote. What is tested is the machinery — what is asked for, what is accepted, what is refused, and what is left alone. Half A's instruction change is in the same position: what is pinned is that the model is told to cite structurally and to keep labels out of the prose, and that the route builds citations from the field when one arrives. **Whether the model actually fills the field, and whether the rewrites read well, are live-model questions this change cannot answer.**

There is **no UI** for the cleanup pass. The Director reaches it through the API, which is the same shape as the VRAM story's unbuilt half and the declared-location field's, and should be read as unfinished rather than implied to be done. That was a deliberate call: another agent was editing `app.js`, `api.js`, `index.html` and `styles.css` in the same working tree throughout.

**Tests.** 28 cases in `tests/test_prompt_cleanup.py`, every geometry assertion made against the Director's real 33-shot plan rather than a tidy invention. Half A: citations built from the field with no label anywhere in the prose; the scan still catching an asset the field omitted, in the right order; a name declared twice and written once cited exactly once; the byte-identity pin; the resolver's id/name/near-miss rules, its de-duplication, and the scan's case-insensitivity and four-character floor at the unit; an old manifest round-tripping. Half B: the report writing nothing and the confirm changing **only `prompt`**, compared as a whole-model dump minus one key through a fresh `ProjectStore` so a field added to `Shot` next month is covered on the day it lands; the whole window fingerprint, the shot count, the manifest order and all six micro-cuts unchanged; every shot's citations byte-identical either side; the Director's harmless "arches sensually on the Dusk Warehouse Bed" rephrased and its neighbour's gutting refused; a clean prompt not sent and not touched; a locked shot and an in-flight render skipped in the existing wordings and not sent; a rendered and an approved shot rewritten, counted and named with their takes and approvals intact; both honest-empty refusals spending no model call; unanswered, duplicated and stray answers reported; the persona and the per-shot labels that actually go on the wire; both geometry guards forced to fire and refusing without saving. `comfy.prompts` is asserted empty on every path. The Director's live manifest is read as evidence and asserted unchanged — SHA-256 `d2b7927b…` before and after.

**28 mutations, 28 killed — and nine of them only after the tests they exposed were written.** The first run reported 13/28, and seven of those "survivors" were the harness's own fault: `app.py`, `models.py` and `director.py` are CRLF while the new module is LF, so every multi-line anchor written with `\n` matched **zero** times and a mutation that was never applied looked exactly like a mutation nothing caught. Asserting the anchor's match *count* is what turned that into a visible failure instead of a false clean bill — the same assertion later caught an anchor that matched **twice**, because `fill_section_looks` ends with the identical `if not request.confirm_apply or not pending: return response`.

The genuine survivors were all one shape: a test that could not distinguish the rule from the data it happened to run on. The resolver's de-duplication is invisible end to end, because `prefer_identity_sheets` collapses duplicate `(asset_id, role)` pairs downstream and silently repairs it. The character exemption is a no-op on this project, because its performer asset is called "HarderFaster · multiview" and no prompt spells the suffix — the case it exists for is an ordinary character named "HarderFaster", which is what a project with no promotion has. No asset in that library is under four characters, and every echo in it is already exact-case, so neither the short-name floor nor the case rule could fail. The window fingerprint was only ever tested by moving a `start`, so a version ignoring `duration` passed. The citation fingerprint's ordering was never exercised, so a set-based one passed. And "a rendered shot is rewritten" was asserted from the *report*, which could not see that a gate refusing rendered shots would simply leave them out of the **payload** and let the double's answer fill the row anyway. Each of those now has an assertion on data that can tell the difference — and the short-name case took two attempts, because the first replacement test used a lowercase word that the (correctly) case-sensitive scan never saw.

The harness re-reads each file immediately before mutating and restores to *that*, rather than to a copy taken at the start, because a second agent was writing the same tree throughout — restoring a half-hour-old snapshot would have silently reverted their work.

## 2026-08-21 — Undo, gap fill, snap to the playhead, and Expand All Prompts where the Director looked for it

Four asks from a Director mid-edit on a 31-shot plan, in their own words:

> *"I accidentally hit split on a clip, but there is no Undo button, so the first shot is split in 2 now."*
> *"When i shorten one clip and leave a gap it would be nice if when i double-click on a shot edge next to that gap if it would extend over to fill that gap."*
> *"Snap to timeline play marker also good so i can line up to beats in the music then snap the Shot edges to it."*
> *"Something i am also not seeing in the timeline button but would probably be fine up by where the Cuts and Snap Cuts stuff are is 'Expand All Prompts'."*

**Every one of these was driven in a real headless Edge before it was claimed** — `tests/e2e_timeline_edit.py`, eight sections, and every assertion reads the **stored manifest off the run's own data root** rather than the browser's copy. That is not ceremony: this project has a recorded incident where substring assertions over `app.js` let three UI guarantees invert with a green suite, and one of the four features below turned out to be structurally dead in a browser while every offline assertion about it passed. See "what a browser found" at the end.

### Undo: a snapshot stack over the shot list, replayed through `PUT /shots`

**The design, and why the cheap one is the principled one here.** The manifest is a single atomic JSON document and the shot list is one field of it, so a snapshot of that field *is* a complete description of the plan at a moment. A command history would need an inverse per gesture, and each inverse would be a second implementation of the rule its gesture already encodes — six more places for split and delete to disagree with themselves. The stack holds up to 40 clones of `project.shots`; a 31-shot list is a few tens of kilobytes.

**Every landed shots save is recorded, not only the six named gestures.** `saveShotsSilently` takes a gesture name (`split`, `delete`, `duplicate`, `add`, `move`, `resize`, plus `gapfill` and `snap` below) and falls back to a generic edit for the inspector's own writes. A stack that skipped those would step back over one silently the next time it was pressed. What an entry restores is `shotsBaseline` — a clone of the list as the server last confirmed it — because the state before save N is by definition the state after save N-1, and holding that centrally is what makes "before this gesture" recoverable for callers that never snapshotted anything.

**Three safety properties, and not one of them is a new rule.**

* **Never undo something that was never applied.** The entry is pushed when the server confirms the write, never when the gesture is made. A refused save leaves the button exactly as shut as it was — asserted in the browser by driving a gesture into a 409 and watching Undo stay dark.
* **Never clobber a state the server changed underneath.** The undo write carries the revision the stack is valid against as `PUT /shots`'s own optimistic-concurrency token. `PROJECT_CHANGED_REFUSAL` — the sentence that route has always answered a stale save with — is now a named constant in `app.py`, used by both manifest writes and copied into `api.js` with a contract test holding the two byte for byte, so the client can say it *before* the request as well as read it after.
* **Never resurrect a shot whose render is in flight.** A snapshot taken before a render carries that shot's pre-render `status`, and `_require_in_flight_status_kept` refuses a body that walks an in-flight status back. Nothing here re-implements that check.

**The server-moved-underneath rule, stated plainly.** The history is valid only against one revision, and it is dropped rather than replayed whenever that revision moves:

* **If this client saw the change** — an approve, a mark-ready, a take swap, a whole-plan sweep, any route reply it adopted — `syncUndoControls` notices `updated_at` no longer matches on the next render, discards the stack, and re-takes the baseline from what is now on screen. The button reads "Nothing to undo", and the *next* gesture is undoable immediately, stepping back onto the plan as that writer left it.
* **If this client did not see it** — a render landing and being reconciled on the server, which moves `updated_at` without moving this copy of it — the write is refused by the route, and the 409's own sentence goes on screen verbatim. The history is then dropped rather than left offering a replay that cannot land.

Refusing is always the answer; there is no branch that reverts on a mismatch. A generation counter closes the last hole: a save queued *before* a discard must not push its snapshot afterwards, because by then `restores` describes a plan from before some other writer rather than from before this click. That branch existed only because mutation testing asked for it — the revision comparison it replaced could no longer fail, since the self-heal had already adopted the new revision by the time the save landed. An untested branch that cannot fire is worse than no branch, and it was deleted.

**Redo fell out of the same machinery** and is shipped: undoing displaces a state, the displaced state goes onto a second stack governed by the identical rule, and a new gesture clears it. Ctrl+Z / Ctrl+Shift+Z / Ctrl+Y, guarded off editable elements by the same check the transport keys use — so Ctrl+Z inside a prompt textarea is still the browser's own text undo — and decided *before* the transport branch so the combination cannot fall through into a one-frame seek. Both buttons live in the bar under the Monitor beside the gestures they step back, and their **accessible name says what will come back** ("Undo the split", "Undo moving a shot"): a glyph is not a label and "Undo" alone does not answer the question the Director had.

**What undo does not cover:** anything that is not the shot list. Documents have their own single-slot restore; approvals, takes and jobs are not in the stack and cannot be stepped back by it.

### Double-click an edge beside a gap

`gapFillPlan` decides everything: which neighbour, how big the gap is, and whether either shot at the resulting cut refuses to have it moved. The neighbour is the **adjacent clip in song order**, never "the nearest one that does not already overlap" — searching for a non-overlapping neighbour is how this gesture would stretch an already-overlapping edge over the clip beside it and take a shot out of the picture without taking it out of the plan.

**A gap is anything above a microsecond**, deliberately not `BOUNDARY_TOLERANCE_SECONDS`. The Director's live plan carries gaps of 0.0017 s, 0.004 s, 0.014 s and 0.015 s — sub-frame residue from hand-dragging edges — every one of them well inside assembly's tolerance and every one of them a gap they asked to be able to close. The edge lands **exactly** on the neighbour rather than near it: no frame quantising, because closing a 0.002 s gap and then re-opening it by rounding to the 1/24 grid is the same defect with extra steps. The song's own head and tail are the boundary when there is no shot there, so the gesture works on the first and last clip.

**Both shots at the resulting cut are asked**, using `timeline.cut_move_refusal`'s three sentences verbatim and in its order — locked, then approved, then rendering — with a contract test holding each against the Python constant. A cut belongs to the two shots that share it, which is `snap_cut_plan`'s own rule rather than a narrower one invented here. `trim_nudge` is left alone even on a rendered shot: closing a 0.002 s gap must not silently re-time a take, and a window that outgrows its take is assembly's judgement to make and refuse, which it already does.

### Snap a cut to the playhead

**The gesture is a magnet on the existing edge drag**, not a new button, because that is what the Director described — park the playhead against the music, then drag the cut onto it — and because it composes with the drag they are already doing. It is measured in **screen pixels** (8 px), not seconds, so it feels identical at every zoom: 0.4 s of pull would swallow a whole short shot at 6 px/s and be unreachably fine at 64. It declines while the song is playing, because a moving playhead is not something an edge can be lined up against. A toggle in the tool bar switches it off; the state is session-only, like the two line mutes beside it, and is announced in words as well as by `aria-pressed`.

**Snapping moves the shared boundary and the freehand drag still does not.** This is the load-bearing half. A shot's edge is its neighbour's edge, and the freehand right-edge drag changes one duration and leaves the next shot's start where it was — which is exactly how the Director's plan came to hold four sub-frame gaps. `boundaryMovePlan` carries the neighbour that meets this edge within `BOUNDARY_TOLERANCE_SECONDS` (assembly's own idea of "the same boundary written twice"); a neighbour further off is on the far side of a real gap and is not touched, because closing that is the double-click's job. Freehand dragging is deliberately unchanged: a behaviour change nobody asked for would be a worse surprise than the gap.

**Nothing here refuses a short window.** The only length check is `duration <= 0`, which is not a window at all. Short windows are legitimate and are being made more so by the concurrent render work, and a minimum invented on this side would forbid from the client exactly what the render path is being taught to allow.

### Expand All Prompts, on the cuts bar

A second affordance for `POST /shots/expand-prompts`, which already existed — the route was verified before anything was built. It shares the route, the label, the help and the refusal with the button in the Director workspace and has its own id because two elements may not carry one. It states what pressing it costs before the click (one model call per shot, and how many shots), and **the route's per-shot report is drawn beside the button that raised it**: what was written, what was locked, what carried render provenance, what was refused, each line in the server's own words under its kind's own name. Those notices already existed in the Director thread, which is two panels away from the button the Director presses — and the route answers per shot precisely because "a locked shot the sweep silently skipped is indistinguishable from one it forgot".

### The shot-length band on the clip

Landed alongside, from the concurrent short-window work's new contract. `readiness_report` answers a third list, `window_warnings`, with a machine-readable `kind` per note, and **the clip colours from the server's verdict rather than from any arithmetic on this side**: the band's constants live in `timeline.py` and the short end's floor fires well below its nominal minimum, so a check re-derived here would paint a clip yellow the server considers fine, or leave one plain that it does not.

Only the **long** end draws a state — the Director's amber, plus the reason appended to the clip's title and accessible name, because colour alone carries no state in this interface. It **warns and refuses nothing**: every control that acts on the shot stays live, asserted in the browser. The **short** end draws nothing at all: the render floors at H3's minimum and centres the window inside it, so a micro-cut is legitimate and colouring one would say "wrong" about something that is fine. Both are still *reported* in the readiness list, under their own labels and never as "Near-duplicate" — `window_warnings` is deliberately a third list because `warnings` already means one thing to every reader it has, and `readinessSummary` still counts one near-duplicate pair rather than three. The clip's UI text does not imply extension is available: it is not, and the note says only that a long take drifts.

### Verification

**Browser-verified** (`tests/e2e_timeline_edit.py`, headless Edge on an isolated port and data root, zero SEVERE console entries beyond one 409 this run drove on purpose):

* Undo restores the **exact** prior plan after split, delete, duplicate, add, move and resize — each driven as a real click, a real confirm dialog or a real pointer drag, each read back off disk and compared field for field. Contiguity re-derived from the manifest after every step.
* A chain six gestures deep, six undos back to the seed, six redos forward, six undos back again.
* The server-moved rule: an outside writer changes the plan, the standing Undo is refused in the server's own words, the split it would have reverted is still there, the other writer's prompt is untouched, and the history is dropped.
* Gap fill on a 0.002 s gap and a 2.5 s one, on both edges, against the song's tail, refused on a locked neighbour with `SNAP_LOCKED_REFUSAL` verbatim, and declined with a named sentence where there is no gap.
* Snap to playhead: the cut lands on the playhead to under 3 ms, the neighbour's start follows to the microsecond, contiguity is unchanged, and the **same drag with the magnet off** lands on the frame grid and leaves the neighbour alone.
* The window band: a clip **dragged** past 15 s takes the amber and the sentence, every edit control stays live, and a 0.6 s shot draws no state while still being reported.
* Expand All Prompts is on the cuts bar, hit-tested, and pressing it raises its own confirmation naming all 12 shots — answered *no*, with the run asserting **zero** requests to the sweep route. No model time and no GPU time was spent anywhere in this work.

**Offline** (`tests/test_frontend_contract.py`, executed under node rather than read): 21 new cases covering the gap rule, the adjacency rule, the three refusals and their order, the pixel tolerance, the shared-boundary move, the collapse guard, the short-window non-rule, every state of both history buttons, the undo write's body and revision on the wire, the discarded-history guard, the double-click window, the band's two kinds, and the sweep report reaching the bar.

**Not verified:** the sweep itself was never run against a model, so what is proven about Expand All Prompts is the affordance, the cost statement, the refusal and the report rendering — not a live expansion. Redo across a page reload does not exist and is not claimed: the history is session state and a reload starts empty.

**Mutation-checked, 23/23 killed.** 21 by the offline suite, and 2 that it structurally cannot kill — the hand-rolled double-click and the pre-drag restore — killed by the browser script. One mutant survived at first and produced a real fix: a snapshot queued before the history was discarded was still being recorded afterwards, which would have offered an undo that reverted another writer's work.

### What a browser found that nothing else could

**The double-click gesture was dead as a `dblclick` listener, and every offline assertion about it passed.** The clip's own `pointerdown` re-renders the SHOTS track, which replaces every clip node in the document — so by the time the second click of a real double-click happens, the element the first one landed on no longer exists and the browser has no common target to dispatch `dblclick` at. The listener was bound, the selector matched, and the event simply never arrived. It is a hand-rolled press-pair now (400 ms, same shot, same edge), which is deterministic and immune to the re-render.

**Found and not fixed:**

* `store.get` and `store.save` open the manifest with no lock between them, and on Windows a read landing inside the save's `os.replace` makes the **save** fail with a 500. The first shape of this script polled the manifest and was therefore breaking the writes it was waiting for; it reads the browser's own resource timings now. The Director's browser polls `/render-status` every two seconds, so this is reachable in normal use, rarely. `store.py` was not this task's surface.
* `#split-shot` silently declines a window under a second. There is no message, so the button simply does nothing — which is the shape of the report that started this work.
* `tests/e2e_shot_controls.py:364` still fails at baseline, pinning a `render-again` tooltip that `RENDER_AGAIN_HELP` does not carry. Untouched here.

## 2026-08-20 — Section looks read out of the Treatment: a dedicated, re-runnable action rather than a second act inside populate

**No live model validated this.** The GPU and the model host were contended by another agent, so every path below was built and tested against a recording double. The prompt wording (`SECTION_LOOKS_PROMPT`) has never been sent to `gemma-4-26b-a4b-it-heretic-ara-v2` or to anything else, and nothing here reports otherwise. What is measured is the *wire schema*, the matching rule and the write guards; what is not measured is how well a local model actually reads a treatment onto a section list.

**The report**, verbatim: *"I clicked on a Section in the timeline and noticed that the shared prompt wasnt pre-filled with information from the Treatment. When expanding the Shot prompts this is supposed to help inform the expansion agent of some of the scene characteristics so it can get the H3 prompt correct but these would be missing in our current project as nothing transferred the appropriate section descriptions into the actual sections once they were populated on the timeline."*

**The cause is structural, not a dropped field.** `SongSection.prompt` is the section's shared look and it is consumed in five places — `reference_prompt` appends it as `"Section look: …"`, `expansion_input` and `shot_expansion_input` carry it into the story pass, `dp_input` into the camera pass, and populate's own instruction quotes it back at the model. On the live project all seven sections held `""`. Those sections were created by the Whisper lyric-alignment pass, so populate takes its `known_sections` branch and never asks the model for structure at all; the one path that would have written a look — `PlannedSection.prompt`, promoted into the constrained grammar the same day — runs **only when the sections are unknown**. Nothing else in the application has ever written that field.

**The design choice: a dedicated action, and populate left alone.** Three arguments, in the order they decided it.

* Populate is the destructive one. It refuses without `confirm_replace`, refuses outright over a lock or an approval, and replaces the whole shot list. Bolting a second model call and a bulk edit of a hand-editable field onto that button means the Director cannot take one without the other.
* This is re-runnable and populate is not. The treatment gets rewritten — that is what the treatment lane is *for* — and the looks want refreshing afterward without the timeline being thrown away to get it.
* Report first, apply on confirm is what this codebase does for every bulk plan change (`populate`'s `confirm_replace`, `snap_timeline_cuts`, asset replacement), and the report is half the feature: *"6 filled, 1 skipped — the treatment does not describe the Bridge"* is the sentence that sends the Director back to the treatment rather than leaving a box mysteriously blank.

`POST /api/projects/{id}/sections/fill-looks`. Without `confirm_apply` it does not call `store.save` and the response carries no `project` at all, so "nothing was written" is visible on the wire. **`overwrite` is a second, separate consent**: confirming *this pass* is not the same act as agreeing to lose a sentence the Director typed into the inspector, so an empty look fills on `confirm_apply` alone and a written one needs both. The report names each written look it would replace with the existing text beside the proposed one, so the trade is visible before it is taken.

**The label-matching rule: by id, corroborated by label, never by position.** The model answers on the `section_id` it was handed — `ExpandedShot.shot_id`'s contract, for its reason — and also copies that section's `label` back; a pair that disagrees is refused and nothing is written. The label is a checksum, not decoration. Section labels come from the lyric sheet's `[Tag]` blocks and repeat by design, the treatment groups them, and the two orders do not agree: the live project's timeline runs Intro, Verse, Chorus, Verse 2, Chorus 2, Bridge, Outro while its treatment runs Intro, "Verse 1 & Verse 2", "Chorus 1 & Chorus 2", Bridge, Outro. Anything positional puts Chorus 2's canopy bed on the Bridge, which is worse than leaving the Bridge empty. A grouped heading is not an ambiguity to resolve — both verses take the verse look, and two sections carrying the same sentence is the correct answer. An id no section has is dropped and counted in the message; a section the model never answered for is left alone and said so.

**The schema requires what the caller cannot proceed without, which is the third instance of the same hole.** `SectionLooks.looks` carries `default_factory=list` and `SectionLook.prompt` carries `""`, so Pydantic left both out of `required`, so LM Studio's constrained decoder would have been free to close the object without either — exactly the shape measured on `DirectorResult.shots` and `PlannedShot.performance`. `section_looks_schema()` promotes both through `constrained_schema`. `prompt` is required **and deliberately has no `minLength`**: required means the decoder must make the model decide about every section, and the empty string staying legal is what keeps "the treatment does not describe this one" sayable instead of forcing a plausible sentence. The chat schema is untouched and its pinned test passes unedited.

**Absent source material is a refusal, not a fabrication.** No sections, an empty treatment, or an empty style bible each refuse with a sentence naming the document to write, **before** the model call — so an unwritten project costs no model time. This is the codebase's absent-analysis convention applied to prose: an empty treatment is *unwritten*, not "no look wanted", the same distinction `shot_vocal_overlap` draws between a measured silence and an unmeasured song. And when every section already has a look with no consent to replace them, the route answers a report without calling the model at all.

**What it produces for the live project's seven, computed read-only.** The manifest was copied to a temp root and the route run there against a double answering the way the treatment reads; `data/projects/project_59f14d19ff10` was opened for reading only and is byte-identical afterwards. All seven fill: Intro takes the moonlit-warehouse establishing paragraph; **Verse and Verse 2 both take the "Verse 1 & Verse 2" paragraph verbatim** and **Chorus and Chorus 2 both take the "Chorus 1 & Chorus 2" paragraph**, which is the grouped-heading case landing correctly; Bridge takes the breakdown paragraph; Outro takes the stillness paragraph. Nothing was written to the live project.

**The browser half**, added last and kept small because another agent was live in `app.js`: one button in the section inspector — where the Director was standing when they found the gap — that reports, shows every section's line in the server's own sentences, and asks the overwrite question only when a hand-written look is in the way.

**Nothing renders, arms, queues or approves.** `comfy.prompts` is asserted empty on every path of every test.

**Tested**: 7 route cases and 2 frontend-contract cases, plus the schema audit extended to the new wire schema. The claim that matters is not that the look was *stored* — the tests assert it arrives in `expansion_input`, in `shot_expansion_input`, and in the string H3 is actually submitted (`"Section look: …"` on the conditioner's prompt), and that it survives a fresh `ProjectStore`. **27 mutations**, each anchored by exact byte count in the CRLF tree, restored with `write_bytes` and SHA-256-compared afterwards across all five touched files: every refusal blanked, the label checksum removed and made case-sensitive, the overwrite guard removed and made absolute, the report path made to write, duplicate ids resolved last-wins, both schema promotions reverted, `minLength` added to the look, the section ids and the treatment dropped from the model's input, and six on the browser half. All 27 caught. One anchor initially matched **four** places in `app.py` and was rejected by the count assertion rather than silently mutating the wrong route — which is the entire reason that assertion exists.

**Suite at this change: 1341 passing, 0 failing** (1332 baseline + 9), `ruff check .` clean, `node --check` clean on `app.js` and `api.js`. `tests/e2e_shot_controls.py:364` fails at baseline and is not part of the default run.

## 2026-08-20 — The video extender: an adapter built from the reachable subgraph, audited against live `/object_info`, and rendered zero times

**Nothing was rendered.** No LTX render was submitted, no GPU time was spent, and no frame from this graph has been measured. The claim this entry makes is that **the payload validates against live `/object_info`** — 43 node classes and five model files, both variants clean on 2026-08-20 — and nothing more. Every readiness line written today says the same, in those words.

**The gap.** `SHOT_MODE_SPECS["extend"]` has shipped with `adapter=""` and `workflow=""` since the shot-mode table existed: plannable, not renderable. Two audited exports had been sitting in `workflow_templates/reference_exports/` unused. The Director's instinct was that a shot window over H3's ~15 s ceiling "may work if fed the last bit of the video we are extending", and that is exactly what the graphs do.

**The Director's framing, which changed the shape of the work mid-flight.** The brief arrived weighted toward lip-sync, on the reasonable ground that the enhancer's `ManualSigmas` had been measured to break it. The ruling: *"its a bit much to assume someone would be singing through a 15 second shot … would be better for a long b-roll cut, but that is up to the user and really is a special case for a music video, but if we ever want to adapt this to do short films to it would be useful to have ready."* So lip-sync is **not a gate**, no machinery was added to protect it, and no refusal encodes it. What was built instead is a clean, general, well-audited extender — because the strategic case is story-based production, where a twenty-second dialogue-free establishing shot is ordinary rather than exotic. The consequence is visible in what is *absent* from `build_ltx25_extend_payload`: no song window, no sync lead, no master-track coupling, no knowledge of shots or takes at all. It takes a path and some numbers.

**`ManualSigmas`, reported as a characteristic.** The base pass runs `1.0, 0.99375, 0.9875, 0.98125, 0.975, 0.909375, 0.725, 0.421875, 0.0` — full noise, eight steps, which is what inventing seconds of video requires. The refine pass, after the latent upscale, runs `0.85, 0.7250, 0.4219, 0.0`. The enhancer starts at 0.909375 and *that* moved lip position, so this graph re-generates at least as hard — and it does so over the reference tail as well as the new seconds, because nothing splices the source's last frames back in: `ImageBatchMulti` takes the head straight off the loader and the tail out of the sampler. The honest reading is that extension suits a long b-roll cut and would visibly disturb a performance shot. That is a note for the user's judgement, recorded in `LTX25_EXTEND_BASE_SIGMAS` and in `docs/WORKFLOW-MAP.md`, and deliberately not a rule the code enforces.

**The reachability finding, which is the one that would have cost a model.** `LatentUpscaleModelLoader` is an **orphan** in the enhancer export and in the audio-replacer export; both adapters correctly leave its file out of their dependency lists, and `tests/test_workflows.py` has pinned that for two adapters. In the video-extension export node `16` genuinely feeds `LTXVLatentUpsampler`, which is the entire low-res-then-upscale shape of the graph. Same class name, opposite answers. A rule of thumb — "that loader is always an orphan here" — would have dropped a model this graph loads, and the pre-flight would then have passed while the render failed at `/prompt`. `reachable_node_ids` decides; a test now asserts all three exports at once, and a mutation that removes the loader from the payload is caught.

**Which export, and why the silent one is not it.** The chosen export has **zero orphans**: 61 nodes, 61 reachable from its saver, so nothing about which nodes to build is a judgement call. Its no-audio sibling reaches 54 of 65, and the 11 orphans are its abandoned audio tail — an edit in progress rather than a finished graph. It also mis-wires `LTXVEmptyLatentAudio.frame_rate` to the "Extend video by (seconds)" constant (10) instead of the framerate constant (24), on **both** instances; transcribing that reproduces a defect and correcting it invents a graph nobody rendered, and neither is acceptable. It saves nothing either: the silent path still loads all five model files — audio VAE included — still runs an audio branch fed with silence, and adds two third-party `easy clean*` VRAM nodes. Recorded as available and audited-second, and used in the suite as the counter-example the reachability walk is shown discriminating against.

**`include_audio` is the one shape control, and its claim is narrow.** True writes the source's own track, normalised to −16 LUFS, with the model's invented continuation after it. False drops the audio decode, trim and concat and the saver's optional `audio` link. It does **not** reproduce the no-audio export, which replaces the audio *conditioning* with an empty latent — saying otherwise would be exactly the unstated substitution this file's docstrings exist to prevent. Either shape needs the take to carry an audio track, since the conditioning tail goes through the audio VAE regardless.

**Substitutions, and a check that proves they were choices.** Six classes are replaced or dropped. `VHS_LoadVideo → VHS_LoadVideoPath` for the reason two adapters already record. `Power Lora Loader (rgthree)` is **dropped rather than substituted** — unlike the enhancer's, this export's node carries no `lora_*` row at all, so it applies nothing and building it would put a node in front of ComfyUI whose only effect is to be there. `SimpleCalculatorKJ` and `ResizeImageMaskNode` go because their schemas carry shapes the shared pre-flight cannot expand — a `COMFY_AUTOGROW_V3` group keyed by `names` with neither a `prefix` nor a `max`, and a `COMFY_DYNAMICCOMBO_V3` sub-input — so their values would have ridden into ComfyUI with the range check silently off. `ImpactImageInfo` and `CM_IntToFloat` fold away. The distinction that matters is invisible from a payload: a graph built around a *missing* node looks exactly like a graph built around an *awkward* one. So `check_substitutions_are_for_schema_shape_not_absence` derives the replaced set from the export and confirms every one of them is registered on the live server.

**Four numbers folded into Python, and the test that keeps the folding honest.** The export computes six in `SimpleCalculatorKJ` nodes. Four are functions of arguments the builder already holds, because `force_rate` pins the loaded rate to the caller's `frame_rate` — so `reference_seconds × rate + 1`, that count over the rate, the same again over the *loaded* rate, and the extension's end time become literals, the way the image-edit adapter resolves the export's Any Switches rather than reproducing them as plumbing. `tests/test_workflows.py` re-derives all four by walking the export's own expressions and constants, **and again at three frame rates the export never carried** — because at 24 fps a rate the adapter hardcoded and a rate it read from the argument produce identical numbers. That second half was added because the mutation run found it missing: hardcoding 24 in place of `rate` survived the first pass.

**Two constants that could have been literals and are not.** `preflight.unbounded_numeric_inputs` reported that `CM_IntBinaryOperation.b` and `CM_FloatBinaryOperation.b` publish neither `min` nor `max`, so the reference window's length would have ridden in with its range check disabled — twice. Both now travel through nodes that do declare a range (`INTConstant`, which is what the export used anyway, and `PrimitiveFloat`). The guard did its job before anything was submitted, which is the second time that particular check has paid for itself.

**Mutation-checked, including the audit.** Fifteen mutations against `workflows.py` and `tests/preflight_ltx25_extend.py`, each anchored by exact byte count and restored by `write_bytes` with the SHA-256 compared afterwards — retuned sigmas, a dropped upscaler, a lost `+1`, a hardcoded frame rate, a disabled ceiling, a mask that truncates instead of padding, a conditioning branch renamed, every one of the five checks blanked, a `DECLARED_LIMITS` row deleted, and a check defined but not wired. All fifteen caught. The audit's own failure path is driven end to end against a **stubbed server** in the suite: a check that reports fails `run_audit` and records no fixture, and blanking any one check turns a red audit green — which is what makes the per-check schema mutations load-bearing rather than decorative.

**Fixture and ledger.** 27 classes recorded, **0 changed** — no already-recorded schema moved. `UNRECORDED_CLASSES` shrank by exactly ten, `LatentUpscaleModelLoader` among them at last, and five more classes joined through `extra_classes` because a check reads them though no payload submits them.

**Deliberately not built: a route, a UI, or a `SHOT_MODE_SPECS` entry.** When extension is offered, and to which takes, is an editorial decision rather than a plumbing one — and AD-12's rule is that a standalone adapter takes the **approved** file where approval exists, which is a caller's judgement, not this builder's. The adapter, its audit and its offline tests are the deliverable.

**Suite at this change: 1332 passing, 0 failing**, `ruff check .` clean, `node --check` clean on `app.js` and `api.js`. Two other agents were landing work in the same tree throughout — an earlier run of this same gate set showed one frontend contract failure and three `ruff` findings in files this change never touched, and both had resolved by the final run — so read the count as a measurement of the tree at one moment and re-run before citing it.

## 2026-08-21 — Replace an asset across every shot that cites it: reported first, applied on confirm

**The report**, verbatim: *"I tried to remove HarderFaster image (Lucy) so it just had the multiview but since its tied to shots it wouldnt let me, its fine that it caught that, but a nice Replace With/Cancel option set would be nice so then i could select another image while i am here in assets and auto replace the one i am trying to remove across the affected shots."* And on the shape of it: *"the replacement should also carry reference_labels, a report would be nice and could include 'already in N shots' which would simply remove the reference its trying to replace and leave its already present reference (this is an actual occurence between HarderFaster image and its multiview in our timeline)."*

**The refusal is untouched and stays.** `DELETE /assets/{id}` still refuses a cited asset by name, with the same sentence, and the Director said plainly that they want it. What was missing was the act it implies. The new `POST /assets/{id}/replace-citations` moves citations and **deletes nothing**: the Director deletes afterwards, through the route that already owns deletion and its file rules. That is deliberate — a route that sometimes deleted and sometimes did not, depending on whether a locked shot happened to be in the way, is a surprise; and an asset one locked shot still cites must still meet the refusal rather than half-vanish from a library that references it.

**Report first, apply on confirm, server-enforced.** `snap_timeline_cuts`' shape, which is `populate`'s `confirm_replace` in a smaller key. Without `confirm_apply` the route does not call `store.save` and the response carries no `project` at all, so "nothing was written" is visible on the wire rather than asserted in prose. The report names three buckets and every skipped shot with its reason.

**The collision rule for `reference_labels`: the label that travels is the surviving citation's own.** One sentence, two readings.

* On a **swap** the survivor *is* the replaced asset's citation, re-pointed, so its label moves onto the new id and wins over anything already stored under it — the replacement was not cited by that shot, so such an entry is an orphan from a citation that no longer exists.
* On an **already-have** merge the survivor is the standing citation of the replacement, so *its* label wins and the removed one goes with its citation. The merge is meant to be purely subtractive; a removal that also renamed the surviving reference would change what an already-authored `h3_prompt` calls that picture in the reference map beneath it.
* The one place a removed label is carried: a merge onto a survivor that has **no** label at all. Nothing is overwritten and nothing is invented, and the Director's word for the subject ("Lucy") survives the promotion to the multiview instead of the shot falling back to the sheet's internal name — which is the very leak the citation-selection change the same day was fixing.
